SCALE-031's small consumer wait and SCALE-098's 48%-longer publication chain are both true and neither is the gap. **What it does not cover, stated rather than papered over**: the gauge sees the ready-spawn path only, not scheduler-slot submissions, so it measures the hot precompute and filter-stripe supply rather than every job class; and a low backlog at a job start proves ready work was absent at that instant, not that a worker was parked at that instant. **Consequence.** The lever is not scheduling, granularity, depth, priority or copy elimination — all closed — but **raising the number of simultaneously independent jobs**, which is a dependency-graph change. `unsafe` does not move it: the graph is the same shape in any language. **Verification.** Gauge env-gated and fully reverted; the tree is docs-only. | **CORRECTION, same session.** The figures above came from a gauge on the `spawn_ready` path only, which is a minority of job starts, and they overstate the incidence badly. Re-instrumenting **both** admission paths (`spawn_ready` and the `admit_ready` drain) over the same 300 frames at eight workers gives 26,839 job starts of which **1,477, or 5.5%, begin with fewer than two ready jobs** — not the 96.3% claimed above. The corrected reading also inverts the conclusion. At those starved starts the scheduler holds a **median of 28 jobs pending on conditions** (max 44), and only **0.8%** of them have no pending work at all: the pool is not out of work, it is holding work that dependencies have not released. That is K10.31's and SCALE-091's "dependency-bound, not supply-gated" conclusion reproduced by direct measurement, not a new starvation finding. **What survives** is the reconciliation of the failed candidates, which does not depend on the incidence: subdividing or re-prioritising work that is already running cannot help when the binding constraint is which conditions have fired. **What does not survive** is any claim that splot lacks submitted work. **Method lesson**: a gauge on one submission path is not a gauge on the scheduler; instrument every path that starts a job before quoting a share.
| SCALE-100 | Investigated; no source change | Attribute SCALE-099's blocked jobs to a dependency class and close the causal chain from the frontier to the eight-worker idle. | **Precompute waiting on cross-frame reference rows is 69% of all dependency wait, and the chain from there to the idle is now complete.** SCALE-099 established the eight-worker idle is dependency-bound, with a median of 28 jobs pending at starved starts, but not which edge holds them. **Method.** Each submitted job is wrapped at its call site with a submit timestamp and a class tag, and reports submission-to-start on entry, so the census needs no scheduler change and no per-condition labelling. Five classes cover every conditional submission in `frame_pipeline.rs`. **Result**, 300 frames at eight workers, total wait by class: **precompute 20,254 ms over 7,359 jobs (2,752 us mean)**, resolve § 7.12 4,116 ms over 2,718 (1,515 us), commit 3,757 ms over 2,710 (1,386 us), frontier 1,144 ms over 3,020 (379 us). Precompute is **69%** of the 29.3 s total. Its conditions are `RowReferenceGate::conditions`, so what it waits on is **the previous frame's published rows**. **The chain closes.** SCALE-098 measured splot publishing a frame's rows over 11.589 ms against dav2d's 7.826; this row shows 24.5 precompute jobs per frame each waiting a mean 2.75 ms on exactly those rows; SCALE-099 shows the resulting instants hold a median of 28 blocked jobs; and the fresh matrix shows 7.09 against 7.73 busy cores at 26.34 against 26.81 core-s. Publication cadence paces precompute admission, which paces occupancy, which is the whole 7.1% wall loss. **This also revises SCALE-098's own reading**: it concluded chain length "is not the occupancy gap" on the strength of SCALE-031's 0.64-0.86 ms/frame consumer-side wait, but SCALE-031 measured the wait of *readers already running*, not the admission wait of precompute jobs that have not started. Both numbers are right; only the second is the binding one. **Target named, with its known hazard.** The inter-link gap is 1032 us and is the chain's own execution: `FrameDeblock::advance` plus window extraction, run **sequentially over three planes** because SCALE-028 found that making them parallel lets Rayon `join_context` steal a commit job and deadlock. Shortening that per-link execution is the lever; SCALE-098 already proved that moving work *onto* the chain (inline stripes, +3.18%) moves it the wrong way. **Verification.** Census env-gated and fully reverted; the tree is docs-only. |
| SCALE-101 | Investigated; no source change | Split the frontier link into its phases, enable plane parallelism on the chain as the maintainer asked, and find what actually paces publication. | **The frontier chain is not the bottleneck: it executes in 179 us of a 1032 us inter-link gap and spends the other 83% waiting for reconstruction.** SCALE-100 named the 1032 us gap as the target on the assumption it was chain execution. It is not. **Phase split**, env-gated timers around the two halves of `ScheduledTileRecon::frontier`, 300 frames at eight workers, 2,980 links: `FrameDeblock::advance` **147.6 us/link** (439.9 ms) and window extraction **31.4 us/link** (93.5 ms), deblock 82.5% of chain work but the two together only **179 us** against SCALE-098's measured 1032 us gap. The frontier therefore sits idle for roughly **850 us per link waiting for the commit spine to finish the next superblock row**. **Plane parallelism is dead twice over.** Asked for directly, it was enabled on `prepare_owned` behind an env gate and **deadlocks**: a 30-frame decode that completes in about 0.25 s had not finished after 300 s and was killed, reproducing SCALE-028's finding that Rayon `join_context` steals a commit job from the chain. And even had it worked it was never the lever — perfect three-way plane scaling of a 147.6 us term inside a 1032 us gap is **9.5%** of publication cadence, so SCALE-028's sequential-plane workaround is costing far less than its prominence suggested. **Where this leaves the mission.** Publication cadence is paced by *reconstruction*, not by the filter chain, and SCALE-100 showed reconstruction's own precompute is 69% blocked on the previous frame's published rows. The two form a recurrence — frame N's precompute waits on frame N-1's rows, which wait on frame N-1's commit, which waits on frame N-1's precompute — and that recurrence, not any single stage, sets the steady-state period. It is SCALE-043's chain-overlap depth (`k` 1.13 against dav2d's 2.11) and SCALE-055's diagonal wavefront restated from the measurement side. **Verification.** Both the phase timers and the plane-parallel gate are reverted; the tree is docs-only. |
| SCALE-102 | Investigated; no source change | Test whether the row-reference gate falls back to whole-frame settling, which would explain SCALE-043's `k` of 1.13, and price what the gate actually asks for. | **Refuted, and cleanly: the gate never falls back and its asks are tight.** `RowReferenceGate::conditions` has a `bounds.settle` branch that abandons row granularity and waits for every named reference to settle, and `RowReferenceBounds::merge` ORs the flag, so one unboundable block in a batch of four would cost that batch all row-level pipelining — a plausible cause of splot's low overlap depth. An env-gated census over 300 frames at eight workers reads **11,174 batches, 100.0% row-granular, 0 whole-frame settles**. The bounds are also not conservative: they come from each block's own resolved `mv` and `motion_compensation_rect`, and the **mean ask is 393 luma rows of a 1080-row frame, 36%**, well under the `128 (r + 1) + reach` worst case, so the gate is already asking for close to the minimum. **What the number then settles.** At SCALE-098's measured publication spans, 36% of a frame is published after **4.22 ms in splot against 2.85 ms in dav2d**, against a steady frame period of about **3.7 ms**: splot's consumer needs its rows later than one period, dav2d's inside it. That is the recurrence stated as a single inequality, and it is why depth sweeps read null — SCALE-023, SCALE-090, K10.16 and K10.19 all added frames in flight to a chain whose links are serially dependent, which on this stream is 91% distance-1 references. **Closes the gate family.** Do not re-open row-gate tightening, settle-flag narrowing or per-batch bound merging: the fallback rate is zero and the asks are near-minimal. The only remaining lever is the publication span itself, which SCALE-101 showed is paced by reconstruction rather than by the filter chain. **Verification.** Census env-gated and fully reverted; the tree is docs-only. |
| SCALE-103 | Investigated; no source change | Split the ordered commit spine and find the serial floor, the last unmeasured stage of the recurrence. | **The spine executes 2.82 ms per frame inside a ~13 ms span, so it is 78% idle, and its serial reconstruction floor is 1.80 ms per frame.** Phase timers inside `commit_active`, 300 frames at eight workers, 41,072 units: publication of precomputed surfaces **192.6 ms (22.7%)**, replay **553.3 ms (66.0%)**, seal **92.3 ms (11.4%)**. **The replay split is the new number.** Units precompute reconstructed in full — **35,392, or 86.2%** — cost **13.6 ms total, 0.38 us each**: replaying an already-finished unit is free, so the spine wastes nothing on them. The remaining **5,680 units, 13.8%**, cost **539.7 ms at 95.0 us each**, which is **18.9 units and 1.80 ms per frame of genuinely serial reconstruction** against a 3.72 ms steady period. SCALE-094 identified what they are: 92.4% general intra, 7.6% unprovable resolve records. **Two candidates die on these numbers before being built.** Moving the 0.31 ms/frame seal copy off the spine onto the frontier chain — which SCALE-101 showed is idle 850 us of every 1032 us link — looks free but is worthless: the spine is **wait-bound, not execution-bound**, so removing execution from it changes nothing, which is exactly why SCALE-098's inline stripe regressed. Tightening the resolve-containment proof to reclaim the 7.6% is worth **0.14 ms/frame**. **The arithmetic that now closes the recurrence.** A batch needs a mean 393 of 1080 reference rows (SCALE-102); at splot's 11.589 ms publication span those arrive at **4.22 ms**, against dav2d's 2.85 ms and a 3.72 ms period. The period is therefore set by how fast the producer publishes the consumer's 36%, not by any stage's execution: spine execution 2.82 ms, frontier execution 179 us per link, and serial floor 1.80 ms all sit under it. **To beat dav2d the publication span must fall from 11.589 ms to about 9.5 ms**, and SCALE-101 showed that span is paced by reconstruction, whose own stages are each already tight. **Verification.** Timers env-gated and fully reverted; the tree is docs-only. |
| SCALE-104 | Committed; retention call open | Parallelise § 7.10.6 TIP output publication, the serial scatter SCALE-093 left unattributed inside `reconstruct_output`. | **The first source win of this series: 10T -1.165% and 8T -0.912%, both 11 of 11 pairs, byte-exact.** SCALE-093 closed TIP after six mechanisms, but its own decomposition stopped at `reconstruct` and never split that stage. A phase census does: of a 4.588 ms median TIP output frame, `refresh_from_references` is 0.740 ms, `prepare_motion_field` 0.327, **`predict_all` 2.921 (63.7%)**, `commit_temporal` 0.405 and `freeze` 0.137 — and only **1.128 ms** of `predict_all` is the compound MC that SCALE-093 measured as already row-parallel over 7 of 8 workers. The remaining **~1.79 ms per frame is `publish_unit_outputs`**, a serial walk over **8,160 units** scattering each unit's finished samples into the frame. **The fix.** On the common path every unit already carries its own samples, so publication reads nothing and each unit writes only its own rectangle. Units tile the frame, so grouping them by luma row gives full-width disjoint bands; `CurrentFrameWorkspace::rect_surfaces` proves the disjointness and hands out one exclusive `CurrentFrameSurface::Rect` per band, which the existing publish path already accepts. The serial loop is kept verbatim for the mixed case where any unit lacks metadata, and for one worker. **Measurements** against exact `6dcaefe39`, `--limit=1000`, rotated alternating pairs: **10T -1.165% (11/11)** against an A/A null of -0.039%, **8T -0.912% (11/11)** against -0.066%, 4T -0.424% (5/5), 2T -0.100% (4/5), 1T -0.379% on a provably unreachable path (`on_multiworker_pool` is false there, so that column is codegen noise, not a gain). No width regresses. Raw 30-frame output is byte-exact at **1/2/4/8/10 workers** (186,624,000 B, SHA-256 `48f0dc14...`) and bare `cargo xtask ci` exits 0. Head-to-head at eight workers moves **1.071x to 1.063x**. **Why SCALE-093 missed it**: its instrumentation timed `reconstruct` as one span and attributed the residue to a scheduling bubble, so the serial scatter inside it was never separated from the parallel MC beside it. **One follow-up built and rejected.** The arena `scratch.output_samples` is cleared and refilled every TIP frame — 8,160 units x 384 samples = 6.3 MB of memset — although every chunk is overwritten by its owning unit and § 7.13.5 publication reads back exactly the `block_w * block_h` samples per plane that prediction wrote, so a chunk's unread tail never reaches the frame. Dropping the refill (truncate-or-grow instead of clear-then-resize) is **byte-exact and deterministic** — three repeated 8T runs plus 1T and 10T all `192e6b3f...` over 200 frames, which is the control stale pooled samples would fail — but it **regresses +0.582% at ten workers, 0 of 11 pairs**, so the fill is not costing what its size suggests. Fully reverted. Do not retry arena fill elimination here. | Maintainer retention call under the 2026-07-31 stacking policy. |
| SCALE-105 | Investigated; no source change | Map every serial section in the decoder by job duration, and calibrate what serial time is worth in wall. | **The first complete map, and it prices the family SCALE-104 opened.** Method: an env-gated timer around each admitted job, bucketed by duration, plus a class tag at each of the seven submission sites so long jobs can be named. **Distribution**, 1000 frames at eight workers: 111,032 jobs totalling 26,158 ms of job time, of which the bulk sits at 256 us to 1 ms (58% of job time). **310 jobs — 0.28% of the population — hold 1,231 ms, 4.7% of all job time**, and those are the serial sections: parse 968.7 ms over 248 jobs (78.7%, median 3.06 ms), TIP output reconstruction 195.7 ms over exactly 45 jobs (15.9%), the per-frame walk 46.9 ms over 17, and single-digit jobs elsewhere. At ten workers with a 1 ms threshold the same map reads parse 1,493.5 ms, precompute 900.8, walk 820.1, TIP 191.8. **The calibration is the useful part.** SCALE-104 removed about 31 ms of serial scatter and returned 41 ms of wall at ten workers, so **serial time inside these jobs converts to wall at better than 1:1** — a serial section does not merely cost its own duration, it drains the pool beside it. That is the pricing rule this ledger lacked, and it is why granularity, priority and depth sweeps all read null while removing one serial scatter did not. **What the map closes.** Parse is 78.7% of the serial family and is irreducible on this stream: one tile, and § 8.2 entropy is sequential by construction, which dav2d pays identically. The per-frame walk is `prepare_scheduled`, whose cost is 135 pooled surface takes plus deblock and filter setup behind a shared pool, so parallelising it trades serialisation for contention. `precompute` appears only because it is numerous, not because any instance is serial. **Two blind alleys closed without building**: the § 7.9 temporal projection already fans out — measured at 17 bands over 46 calls, `on_multiworker_pool` true — and SCALE-102's row gate has no fallbacks. **Verification.** All timers env-gated and fully reverted; the tree is docs-only against `0949ebb8b`. | **One hypothesis refuted here rather than left open**: since a single tile with `context_update_tile_id = 0` publishes CDFs only at its own parse end (SCALE-054), the parse chain looks like a strictly serial floor across all frames. It is not. Summing every parse job over 955 frames gives **4,596 ms against a 4,131 ms wall on the same instrumented run** — the sum exceeds the wall, so parse jobs demonstrably overlap each other and § 8.2 constrains only the CDF publication point, not the whole parse job. Do not treat Sigma-parse as a lower bound on the decode.
| SCALE-106 | Investigated; no source change | Re-sweep pipeline depth after SCALE-104, and state exactly what separates splot from dav2d in one arithmetic. | **Depth is already optimal, and the whole remaining task is +0.61 busy cores.** Frame-delay sweep at ten workers, `--limit=1000`, three runs each: `auto` (= 10) and 8 both land at **3.55 s**, 12/16/20 at 3.57, 6 at 3.67 and 4 at 4.12. That wait is not a removable cost: the motion recurrence itself — resolve 0.474 ms plus a band-0 precompute of about 1.2 ms plus the fold — closes in well under the 3.56 ms steady frame period, so a frame submitted 9.6 ms before its turn is simply admitted early while the pool works frames N-1 and N-2. **There is therefore no removable cost anywhere in the 17.272 ms prefix**: parse 1.119, walk gates 0.210, resolve execution 0.474, and the rest is pipeline turn. This closes the successor this row named and, with it, the prefix as a family. **One scalar candidate built and rejected on the assembly gate.** `gdf::compute_enabled_segment` is the profile's largest exclusive leaf at 1.98%, and its inner loop repeats three `checked_add` plus error arms per 8- or 16-sample chunk although `output_row` and `class_row` are row-invariant. Hoisting them to one span validation per row is byte-exact at 1/8/10 workers (`192e6b3f...`) but **grows the function 1,791 to 1,820 instructions** and measures -0.112% (4/7) at one worker, inside noise. The hoist adds row-level checks without removing the inner ones: `exact_slice` and the `try_from` array conversions carry their own length checks, and LLVM does not connect a row span to a chunk index through the loop. It measures **+0.020% (4/9) at one worker and +0.208% (2/9) at ten**: null. The arithmetic explains it: 9 instructions of 1,791 is 0.5% of a function that is 1.98% of the profile, so 0.01% overall, below any instrument here. **The bounds checks are not what this kernel costs; the filter arithmetic is** — which is what K10.24 concluded for the GDF family from the other side. Two results. First, `walk_frame`'s own gates — the motion-metadata conditions, the reconstruction-lane reservation and the temporal baton — bind for only **0.210 ms**, so lane depth is not the pipeline's constraint, which is consistent with SCALE-090's null sweep and today's null frame-delay sweep and refutes reading SCALE-043's lane-gate observation as a cost. Second, **the segment (3) would compress is 1.796 ms of a 17.272 ms prefix, 10%**, and it is followed by an 11.858 ms gate that binds after it, so compressing it returns nothing — the same conclusion the parse-share argument reached, now measured segment by segment rather than inferred. **The real term is the 11.858 ms between `walk_frame` starting and the frame's first precompute being submitted**, which is the § 7.12 resolve chain waiting on the previous frame's motion bands. That is 69% of the prefix and larger than every candidate this ledger has priced since SCALE-100. Its decoupling was tried twice — SCALE-018 and SCALE-027, the latter measured at **+1.083%** and reverted — so it is not open as stated, but no row has ever priced the *whole* segment, only the projection slice inside it. **That is the named successor.** **CORRECTION (SCALE-111).** This row predicted the row-granular handoff would be zero on the full stream, from a per-frame prefix census. The census was not wrong about its own term -- parse really does finish 13.9 ms before the frame's first precompute is submittable -- but it measured a frame's prefix in isolation and so could not see the driver-side effect: `promote_typed` blocked the driver thread in `wait_with_pool_assist` until each parse job returned, and promoting off the pass's pre-parse half releases it. That mechanism is a hypothesis; the wall-clock result is a measurement. **Do not price this family from a prefix census again -- and do not conclude neutrality on a scheduling change from `--limit=600` or shorter, where this same change reads inside the noise.**

At **10T**: base 11.347 s, LTO 11.103 s, dav2d 11.009 s -- LTO vs base **-2.074% [-2.238, -1.755], 6/6**, base vs dav2d **+3.14%** (not the +4.50% the cross-session figure implied), leaving splot+LTO **+0.85% short**. The other widths (1T -17.03%, 2T -9.27%, 3T -12.05%, 4T -10.36%) are each internally paired and stand, but are n=5, where a sign test cannot go below p=0.0625 for any single width; the aggregate 30/30 is p=1.9e-9. Its mechanism is measured, not assumed: the crate split puts every hot kernel in `splot-recon` and every driver in `splot-decode`, so fat LTO deletes a non-inlinable edge — TLS subpel-scratch accessors 1.98% -> 0.00%, `u16_samples_exceed` call overhead -0.87pp, CGU dedup stubs -0.50pp, with merged kernels' shares conserved. **Sync time is unchanged (base 5.62% of work, LTO 5.59%), so this buys work and not occupancy and stacks with any future occupancy fix.** **Correction to SCALE-066: hosted CI *is* affected.** `.github/workflows/ci.yml` builds the release profile twice (`cargo test -p splot-decode --release --lib`, `cargo build --release -p splot-cli`), so every PR pays the 26 s -> 1 m 49 s local cost; ci.yml's own comment already says release "enables fat LTO", so it was authored for this state. `cargo xtask ci` exit 0. **Three candidate families closed by measurement.** (a) **Dependency edges: null.** Env-gated submit->start attribution by condition class at 8T/10T over 3,000 frames puts precompute at 58.8% of all wait, but that wait is **100% cross-frame and already row-granular**, and `precompute_settle` — the coarse whole-frame path — has a count of **exactly 0**. There is no coarse gate left to unwind. Recorded slack is 308 s against ~18 cpu-s of real idle, so wait share is graph slack, not wall attribution, and no single edge holds the gap. (b) **`commit(0)` gated on `filters_ready`/the parse tail**, which no prior row names (0 hits): dead — commit(0) provably reads none of `attach_filters`' output, and `prepared[0]` lands ~13 ms later (SCALE-110). Not built. (c) **Scalar leaves: withdrawn.** The profile is flat (no leaf >=5%) and the candidates *halve* from 1T to 10T (`classify_grid_from_cache` 4.71 -> 2.03%, `filter_luma_segment_u16` 4.84 -> 2.89%), cutting their ceilings to 0.07-0.18%. **A width sweep then explains why ten workers specifically is where it fails, and refutes the obvious hypothesis.** Against dav2d the retained binary reads 6T -5.131% (5/5), 8T -1.753%, **9T -0.524% (4/5, still a win)**, 10T +0.836% (0/5), 12T +1.536% (0/5). This host is 8P+4E, so a P/E-core cliff would show at nine workers and **it does not** -- degradation is smooth and monotone, matching the standing occupancy-deficit finding of no cliff at eight. The mechanism is arithmetic: the **occupancy ratio is near-constant at 0.93-0.96 at every width**, while splot's **CPU advantage decays** (-8.83% at 6T, -7.06% at 8T, -6.70% at 9T, -5.79% at 10T, -2.55% at 12T) as its own CPU inflates going wide. splot wins while the CPU advantage exceeds the occupancy deficit and loses when it does not; the crossover sits between nine and ten workers. **Closing 10T therefore needs either less CPU inflation at width or better occupancy, not a removable dependency edge -- and the census found no such edge.** **The last build-config lever was then tried and rejected.** `.cargo/config.toml` pins `target-cpu` for `x86_64` only, leaving aarch64 at rustc's default, and `apple-m2` adds `bf16`, `bti`, `ecv`, `i8mm`, `v8.5a` and `v8.6a`. Built byte-exact, it measures **+0.173% at ten workers [95% CI -0.079, +0.290], 2/6 — null — and +0.212% at eight [CI +0.202, +0.506], 0 of 6, i.e. slightly *worse* with the interval excluding zero.** The added features are int8-matrix and bfloat, which u16/i16/i32 kernels cannot use, so there was no mechanism for a gain; it would also drop M1 support for nothing. Not adopted, and `.cargo/config.toml` was never modified (measured through `RUSTFLAGS`). **The comment in that file claiming aarch64 needs no floor bump is now measured rather than assumed.** **Still open: 10T by 0.83%** (a third independent triple reads dav2d vs splot **-0.826% [CI -0.929, -0.716], 6/6** at ten workers and **+1.836% [CI +1.765, +2.110], 6/6** at eight). **The candidate space this campaign can reach is now exhausted: dependency edges are null by census, scalar leaves are withdrawn on their own ceilings, TIP admission failed its pre-declared occupancy gate, `panic = "abort"` is barred by the `output/raw.rs` fail-closed path, and both remaining codegen levers are measured. Closing ten workers requires attacking CPU inflation at width or occupancy directly — the architectural target this ledger has pursued since SCALE-100 — not another candidate of this size.** **Finally, the mission's OWN benchmark contract was measured, which this campaign had not done — every number above is the `--limit=3000` steady-state arm, while the contract at the top of this file is `--limit=30`. On the contract, three-way rotated (dav2d / base / LTO), n=21, paired medians with bootstrap CIs, splot+LTO **wins all five contract widths**: 1T **-27.453% [-27.591, -27.407] 21/21**, 2T **-22.195% [-22.330, -22.001] 21/21**, 4T **-19.290% [-19.448, -19.087] 21/21**, 8T **-9.184% [-9.594, -9.047] 21/21**, 10T **-5.218% [-6.292, -4.628] 20/21**, against an A/A null of +0.113% [-0.655, +1.381] on the same short arm. LTO improves the contract at every width as well (10T base -2.885% -> LTO -5.218%). **Both arms must be quoted together, as SCALE-107 requires: on the 30-frame contract splot wins 1/2/4/8/10; on the 3000-frame steady-state arm it wins 1/2/3/4/6/8/9 and still loses 10T by 0.83% and 12T by 1.54%.** The contract is startup-weighted and the long arm is steady-state; they measure different things and both results are real. **Two further candidates were then opened on maintainer challenge and both are closed by measurement.** (a) **CCSO chroma vectorization — premise falsified.** `ccso_simd_row` (`filters/ccso.rs:445`) bails at line 456 on `sub_x != 0`, so on this 4:2:0 stream the existing 16-lane `std::simd` kernel never runs for U/V, and CCSO is 1.81% of CPU at 10T by `SPLOT_DECODE_TIMING`. The `sub_x == 1` deinterleaving path was built (41/-13, byte-exact `0b4e35d3...` at five widths) and is **worthless: instrumenting `prepare_ccso_plane` counts 1210 luma plane-instances against 43 + 32 chroma, and chroma carries a quarter the pixels, so chroma CCSO is about 1.5% of CCSO work = 0.03% of CPU.** The 1.81% is essentially all luma, which is already vectorized; `ccso_units_ms` moved 1731.8 -> 1738.2, null. Reverted. **It did surface a real pre-existing correctness defect, filed separately: the luma path ignores the tile clamp in both directions — it guards `x_start + min_dx < 0` instead of `< min_luma_x`, and on the `bo_only` path never checks `x_end - 1 <= max_luma_x` — so with `disable_loopfilters_across_tiles` and more than one tile column the vector path reads across the tile edge where the scalar path clamps.** (b) **Bounding the `FrameProgress::workspace` exclusive hold — dead by construction.** Stack-attributed sampling made this the largest sync term (2.00% of worker wall, 2.22 s/run) and the only one scaling super-linearly with width (`hold_reference_slot` 0.300% at 4T, 0.971% at 8T, 1.847% at 10T), because `PublishedFrame::drop` runs the stripe copy inline on the reader's thread under the write lock. Capping `copy_queued` to one stripe measured **+0.41% at 10T**, and env-gated probes explain why: **the batch-size histogram is 2687/41270/2991/300/200/... — 92.5% of drains already copy exactly one stripe**, so the cap is a no-op and the diff is pure overhead. The single-drainer variant is equally dead: 79,926 of 124,551 drain attempts already fail `try_write` and return immediately, about 4 ms total. Total exclusive hold is **1.03 s** (drain 0.588, freeze 0.434, blocking 0.008) against **2.09 s** of reader wait, and the 0.434 s freeze is the frame handoff. **The reader wait is the cost of the lock existing, not of over-holding it; removing it is the ownership redesign SCALE-097 prices at +48.7%.** Both reverted. **The scheduler ready lock was independently re-confirmed at 0.062% of worker wall with `AdmissionScheduler::submit` absent from the profile, matching the earlier amplification result.** splot uses 5.6% less CPU there (95.25 vs 100.88 core-s) at 8.56 busy cores against 9.15; at dav2d's occupancy its own CPU finishes in 10.41 s and wins by 5.5%. The one structurally coarse edge left is **TIP output frame admission** — 88 frames in 3,000, whole-frame `settled_condition` + `field_condition` while every ordinary inter frame gets row watermarks, median submit->start **23.07 ms at 10T** — but 23 ms is ~6 frame periods at the 3.94 ms cadence, the same signature SCALE-110 proved was pipeline turn rather than cost, and SCALE-109's neighbouring build measured exactly null. **That control was then built and run, and the edge fails it.** Sampling worker state inside all 88 TIP submit->start windows against 51 duration-matched control windows at ten workers gives **median starved workers 0.00 in TIP windows** — the pre-declared go line was "under ~1 worker and the edge is dead" — against median running 9.00 and median idle 1.00. The generous reading does not rescue it: TIP windows are only **+0.314 workers** more starved on the mean than matched controls (2.146 against 1.832; whole-run 1.205), which over 1.746 s of total window time is **0.548 worker-seconds, about 0.5% of wall at ten workers even if perfectly recovered**, against a 1.0% gap. **TIP output admission is closed by its own control**, and with it the last structurally coarse edge in the census. The 10T deficit is not held by any single dependency edge; it is distributed occupancy, and closing it needs a different instrument than submit->start wait. Instrumentation fully reverted. |
| SCALE-114 | **Retained: plane reclaim on drop, and it wins the last width** | Ask the one question the copy family had never been asked — not "can the copies be deleted" (SCALE-097 +50.7%, direct write +48.7%) but **"are more bytes copied than necessary"** — and follow it wherever it leads. | **A byte census found a refcount defect that only bites under threading.** Counting actual bytes at every copy and fill site puts the decoder at **41.7 MB of copies and 13.3 MB of `memset` per 6.22 MB frame** at ten workers, and the fills are almost entirely a going-wide cost: **0.050 frames-of-fill per frame at one worker against 2.236 at ten**. Attribution found the cause. `reclaim_unowned_frames` guards on `SharedFrame::handle_count`, the **frame** `Arc`'s refcount, while the reclaim it performs requires uniqueness of a **different** `Arc`, the `CompletionCell`, which a shared slot handle keeps alive. The two diverge exactly when frames are shared, so plane reclaim succeeds **295 times in 300 at one worker and 1 time in 300 at ten** — nine planes taken per frame, six returned, three freshly allocated and zero-filled every frame. **This is a resource defect that worsens as threads are added, not a tuning artifact.** `Drop for DecodedFrame` now returns the buffers at refcount zero, which covers every release path; the bespoke `reclaim_planes` chain is deleted, `TileDecodeScratch::take_surface` stops re-filling recycled surfaces, and retention is budgeted in **total samples rather than a fixed plane count** so it adapts with resolution, bounds only retained memory, and places **no ceiling on concurrency** — the maintainer's standing constraint is that threads must scale to 128. Fills fall **83%**. **Rotated paired medians, bootstrap 95% CIs, `--limit=3000`**: 10T **-0.735% [-0.879, -0.537] 14/15**, 8T **-0.894% [-1.007, -0.625] 8/8**, 4T **-0.773% [-0.862, -0.736] 8/8**; 1T costs **+0.086% [+0.041, +0.142]** because the reclaim path already worked at one worker, so no fills are recovered and only drop-side bookkeeping remains. **Stacked with SCALE-113's profile change this wins every contract width against dav2d**, each CI excluding zero: 1T **-16.910%**, 2T **-9.887%**, 4T **-11.107%**, 8T **-2.545%** (all 8/8) and **10T -0.179% [-0.225, -0.039], 15 of 20, on 6.7% less CPU (11.000 s against 11.016 s)** — the width every candidate since SCALE-100 had failed to take. **Correctness was established by poisoning rather than by hoping.** Filling every recycled buffer with a sentinel instead of zero before handing it out is byte-exact at 1/2/4/8/10 over 3000 frames **and** passes the AVM-hash fixture corpus at threads 1 and 8 with 31.3 M poisoned samples recorded, so the test is non-vacuous and no consumer reads an uninitialised sample; the shipped build is byte-exact `0b4e35d3...` at the same five widths, `decoder_oracle` passes under release codegen, `cargo xtask ci` exit 0. **Rejected on the way, each by measurement**: CCSO chroma vectorization (the `sub_x` guard really does keep the 16-lane kernel off both chroma planes, but chroma is 75 plane-instances against luma's 1210 at a quarter the pixels, so the ceiling is 0.03% of CPU, not the 1.81% the phase timer suggested); bounding the `FrameProgress::workspace` exclusive hold (**92.5% of drains already copy exactly one stripe**, so the cap is a no-op and measured +0.41%); `target-cpu=apple-m2` (+0.173% at 10T, worse at 8T, and its `i8mm`/`bf16` additions are unusable by integer kernels); restoring `#1458`'s concurrency caps (**-0.870% at 10T and byte-exact, but rejected by the maintainer on architecture: a `min(3)` ceiling cannot scale to 128 threads** — and the isolation showed the win was not the cap limiting concurrency at all but `promote_typed`'s `wait_with_pool_assist` making the driver assist the pool sooner, which is the wrong mechanism to buy with a constant). **Also surfaced and filed separately, not fixed here: `ccso_simd_row`'s luma path ignores the tile clamp in both directions, so with `disable_loopfilters_across_tiles` and more than one tile column the vector path reads across the tile edge where the scalar path clamps.** |
| SCALE-115 | **Retained: the blocked driver assists the pool** | Recover the 0.65% the rejected `#1458` cap was actually buying, without any cap — the maintainer's standing rule is that threads must scale to 128. | **The cap's win was never concurrency limiting, and the isolation proved it.** Restoring `#1458`'s `min(3)` entropy bound measured **-0.870% at 10T (6/6)** and, isolated, `-0.651% (15/15, CI [-0.908, -0.573])` for the entropy bound alone against `-0.251%` for the lane bound. **Rejected on architecture regardless** — a `min(3)` ceiling cannot scale to 128 workers — but the mechanism turned out to be `promote_typed`'s `wait_with_pool_assist` making the driver assist the pool *sooner*, not the pipeline being throttled. **With `--threads=N` the process runs N+1 threads and the driver is parked for essentially the whole run**, which is why splot reaches 8.56 effective cores at ten workers against dav2d's 9.15 while spending 6.7% *less* CPU. **The fix is structural rather than numerical.** `drain_ready_entropy` promotes a strict prefix and stops at the first unsettled entry; at that moment the driver's own next step is blocked on a parse job it does not own, so it now donates that blocked time through the existing `assist_installed_pool` primitive, one queued job at a time, stopping the instant the front settles or the pool reports nothing runnable. Nothing is counted and nothing is bounded: `limit` stays `ring.capacity()` so entropy depth and concurrency still scale with worker count, and the predicate reads live pool state rather than a queue length. `assist_pool_once` is the **nonblocking** half of `assist_pool_or_park` — it never parks, so there is no lost-notification window and none of the SCALE-028/101 failure mode, which needs a blocking wait a stolen job must satisfy. Arbitrary pool jobs already ran on the driver at this call site via `wait_with_pool_assist`; this changes how often, not where. **Rotated paired medians, bootstrap 95% CIs, `--limit=3000`, against a 10T A/A control of -0.064% [-0.123, +0.083]: 1T -0.147% [-0.250, -0.070] 6/6, 4T -0.222% [-0.303, -0.186] 10/10, 8T -0.268% [-0.340, -0.080] 12/12, 10T -0.257% [-0.336, -0.124] 14/15 — every width improves**, and the 1T gain cancels the +0.086% SCALE-114 cost there. It recovers about 40% of the cap's figure; the rest came from the cap *also* halting driver submission, which is the throttle that was rejected and is not recoverable demand-driven. **Stacked final matrix against dav2d, `--limit=3000`, every CI excluding zero: 1T -17.059% (8/8), 4T -11.357% (8/8), 8T -2.819% (12/12), 10T -0.236% [-0.363, -0.087] (11/12) on 6.8% less CPU.** Byte-exact `0b4e35d3...` at 1/2/4/8/10 over 3000 frames, `decoder_oracle` green twice under release codegen, `cargo xtask ci` exit 0, workspace suite run a second time standalone with no hang. **The recycling-defect class that produced SCALE-114 is now exhausted, checked rather than assumed.** Hit/miss/return counters on all twenty pool sites at 1T against 10T show every pool at or above 96% hit rate at ten workers except the shared quant pool, and the long tail moves from exactly 1 miss at 1T to exactly 10 at 10T — one cold start per worker, healthy by construction. **No second refcount-vs-requirement mismatch exists.** Allocations do rise 5.7x going wide (182 to 1041 per frame, 1.74 to 8.85 MiB), and 52% of the bytes come from the unpooled banded temporal projection, but a 10x amplification prices **the entire allocation class at 0.19% of wall and that path at 0.057%**, so pooling it is a declared NO-GO against its own 0.30% bar. **What the census did find is five fixed-count retention caps that violate the scale-to-128 rule** — `MAX_RETAINED_SHARED_QUANT_BUFFERS = 2_560`, `MAX_RETAINED_RECON_ROW_BUFFERS = 512`, `MAX_RETAINED_STRIPE_BUFFERS = 128`, `MAX_RETAINED_WINDOW_BUFFERS = 64` and a bare `recycler.len() < 32` — with the quant pool **already saturating at ten workers, rejecting 22.4 returns per frame**; raising it to 1,000,000 in a control drove rejected returns to zero and halved miss bytes. That is scalability hygiene worth about 0.001% of wall and must be landed without a perf claim. |
| SCALE-116 | **Retained: five retention bounds follow pool width; campaign matrix closed** | Retire the fixed-count retention caps the SCALE-115 census exposed, on the maintainer's standing rule that the decoder must scale to 128 workers, and close the campaign with a definitive matrix. | **A census with every bound raised to 1e6 shows demand is linear in pool width at all five sites**, so a fixed count is structurally wrong rather than merely small: shared quant peaks **502 / 4,374 / 6,974** retained at 1/4/10 workers against a cap of **2,560**, and recon-row sets **0 / 499 / 1,273** against **512**. The consequence is already live at ten workers: the quant pool **rejects 40.3 returns per frame**, 50.8 takes per frame find every bucket empty, and its hit rate falls **99.54% -> 85.89%**. Each bound becomes `current_pool_width() * PER_WORKER` floored at today's constant — `#1458`'s existing `max_retained_deblock_grids()` idiom, no new helper. **They bound retained MEMORY only**: all five are recycle-side, every `take` keeps its allocate-fresh fallback, and none can deny a buffer, block, or serialise a caller, so nothing here throttles concurrency; the quant pool caches its limit and re-derives it only on the branch that used to reject, keeping the width query off the per-coeff-block path. Rejected returns at ten workers go to **zero** for both saturating pools (12,083 -> 0 quant, 1,142 -> 0 recon row over 300 frames) and **peak RSS is flat** — 154/443/803 MB before against 155/442/797 MB after at 1/4/10 — because the concurrent workload allocates these buffers regardless and the caps only forced free-and-realloc churn. **Timing is neutral, which is this change's bar, and the short-run reading was correctly not banked**: `--limit=600` read -0.401% at 10T, but at the campaign's `--limit=3000` it is **-0.134% [95% CI -0.304, +0.027], 10/15 — CI crossing zero**. One bound is extrapolated rather than measured and is flagged in the commit: the window pool's demand is flat over 1-10 workers, so its 8-per-worker slope needs a census at a width where it actually saturates. **Definitive closing matrix against dav2d, `--limit=3000` (1T/2T at 1000), rotated paired medians with bootstrap 95% CIs — every width won, every interval excluding zero: 1T -16.960% [-17.008, -16.912] 8/8, 2T -9.695% [-10.117, -9.523] 8/8, 4T -11.379% [-11.394, -11.325] 8/8, 8T -2.899% [-2.991, -2.721] 12/12, 10T -0.480% [-0.670, -0.368] 12/12 on 6.9% less CPU.** Ten workers moved from **+4.501% (0/6) at campaign start to -0.480% (12/12)**, a five-point swing, against a start-of-campaign A/A null of +0.087%. **The four retained changes are four distinct mechanisms** — a build-config inlining barrier (SCALE-113), a refcount guard that tested the wrong `Arc` (SCALE-114), an idle N+1th thread (SCALE-115) and retention bounds that ignored worker count (this row) — and **none was found by profiling**, because each one's cost surfaces as anonymous `memset`, `memmove` or park time rather than as a named hot leaf. **The transferable method is to measure a resource path's behaviour at one worker against ten and treat a collapse as a defect, then prove any removed initialisation unread by POISONING it with a sentinel and reporting the poisoned-sample count so the test is provably non-vacuous.** |
| SCALE-120 | **Retained by maintainer direction: generic 8/10-bit source SIMD with direct strided `u16` publication** | Remove packed loop-restoration output staging for the highest-volume remaining call site while preserving and sharing the 10-bit SIMD path. | **The staging prize is 8-bit-only, but the kernel body now serves both storage widths.** The 3,000-frame 10-bit mission stream made 216,386 loop-restoration calls and every one already wrote directly into its strided `StripePlane`; staged and row-copy bytes were exactly zero. The decoder corpus at 1T/8T found 488 staged 8-bit calls. Wiener NS chroma was the largest shared site at **328 calls / 1,159,168 samples**, versus luma 114 / 1,089,536 and PC-Wiener 20 / 108,544. A source-generic 64-lane SIMD body now takes a width-specific `u8`-widening or `u16` loader and writes both source types into the same validated strided-`u16` destination. The decoder obtains that exact rectangle through `StripePlane::rect_mut`; validation of the complete destination and both padded sources precedes the first write; lossless replacement, error order, arithmetic and scheduling boundaries are unchanged; and the packed API remains the compatibility fallback. Every chroma corpus call moves to direct publication. Per corpus pass this eliminates **579,584 bytes of packed output, 579,584 bytes of copy reads, and 1,159,168 bytes of destination writes**; the 10-bit mission stream remains at **0 removed bytes per frame** because it was already direct. **Affected-kernel release microbenchmark**, 256x56 8-bit 4:2:0 with active coefficient extrema, nine alternating 100-call reps: old packed scalar-plus-copy median **202.7 us/call**, direct SIMD **32.5 us/call, -83.9%**. **10-bit regression matrix**, exact-base A/B medians at 1/2/4/8/10 workers: wall **-0.140/-0.326/+0.089/+0.281/-0.548%**, CPU **-0.207/-0.144/+0.133/-0.053/+0.160%**, all inside A/A wall **+0.285/-0.249/-0.023/-0.187/-0.784%** and CPU **+0.368/-0.079/-0.055/-0.109/+1.139%**. Exact raw output matches base at 1T/10T (186,624,000 bytes, SHA-256 `48f0dc14...`), all 278 strict fixtures match the AVM hashes at 1T/8T, and focused parity/extrema/stride/terminal/fail-before-write/mutation tests pass. Assembly keeps bounds guards in the row preheader and has no new inner-loop output branch or vector spill: the existing 10-bit kernel shrinks **1,253 -> 1,239 instructions, 62 -> 60 branches, 28 -> 26 calls, 132 -> 130 stack references**; the separate new 8-bit instantiation is 1,261 instructions / 60 branches / 26 calls / 148 stack references. The prior 16/32-lane 10-bit experiments remain rejected; 64 lanes are shared without changing the existing arithmetic. |

## Rejected experiments

- Reconstruction lane width 4 and width 10.
- Split entropy/reconstruction at 2 workers without frame-context storage
  ownership; it improved 2T by 17% but exceeded the serial reference-store
  peak.
- Scheduler-owned whole-frame entropy with three pending contexts; exact, but
  only 2.8% faster at 10T and neutral at 1T.
- Owned four-unit commit baton with lazy global reconstruction windows of 9,
  18, and 27 batches; exact, but slower or neutral because it did not advance
  reference/filter publication.
- LIFO in place of FIFO task scopes.
- Batch size 1.
- Commit fusion with blocking and try-lock ownership.
- Commit-gated bounded precompute window.
- Removing the temporal scratch chain.
- Exact whole-field motion dependency selection.
- Motion-band publication plus ordered band resolve without filter/reference-row
  publication; exact, but only 1.9% faster at 10T and 2.9% at 8T.
- Canonical reconstruction bands plus scheduled deblock/filter stripes without
  the adjacent entropy, motion, and reference-row stages; exact, but only 1.8%
  faster at 10T and neutral/slower at 1T and 8T.
- Removing motion-field folding/locking as a ceiling probe.
- The earlier cross-stage deblock slab scheduler.

These results reject the specific implementations, not the architectural tasks.
Do not repeat them without a materially different ownership model and a stated
reason the prior failure no longer applies.
